You are an expert JAX/TPU kernel engineer. Write the inner tile of a grouped matmul (MoE `gmm`) Pallas kernel for TPU.

## What you write, and what you do NOT write

This is a fill-in task with a real seam in it. **The harness owns the
interface; you own the strategy.** The harness has already written the exclusive scan of `group_sizes` to row offsets, the group-ALIGNED row permutation (so no row tile ever straddles two experts -- the zero-size-group and boundary-tile correctness traps are the harness's), the `pallas_call` with scalar-prefetched per-tile expert ids, every `BlockSpec`, and the un-permute of the result. That
code is fixed, it is shown to you verbatim below, and your job is only to
supply the named functions it calls.

Your code is pasted ABOVE the scaffold into one module and submitted as a
single program, so the entrypoint, the shapes and the judge contract are
already satisfied before you write a line. What is NOT decided is every choice
that changes the number the judge measures.

## The scaffold your code is appended to (verbatim, exactly as submitted)

```python
# ===================== HARNESS SCAFFOLD -- appended verbatim =================
# Owns: the exclusive scan of group_sizes to row offsets, the group-ALIGNED row
# permutation (so no row tile ever straddles two experts -- the zero-size-group
# and boundary-tile correctness traps are the harness's, not yours), the
# pallas_call with scalar-prefetched per-tile expert ids, every BlockSpec, and
# the un-permute of the result.
import jax as _jax
import jax.numpy as _jnp
from jax.experimental import pallas as _pl
from jax.experimental.pallas import tpu as _pltpu


def _ru(x, m):
    return ((x + m - 1) // m) * m


def _tiles(m, k, n, g):
    bm, bk, bn = choose_tiles(int(m), int(k), int(n), int(g))
    bm = min(max(_ru(int(bm), 8), 8), 1024)
    bk = min(max(_ru(int(bk), 128), 128), int(_ru(k, 128)))
    bn = min(max(_ru(int(bn), 128), 128), 2048)
    return bm, bk, bn


def kernel(lhs, rhs, group_sizes):
    m, k = lhs.shape
    g, _k, n = rhs.shape
    bm, bk, bn = _tiles(m, k, n, g)
    np_ = _ru(n, bn)
    # static upper bound on the group-aligned row count: every expert wastes at
    # most bm-1 rows, and a zero-size expert wastes none.
    mp = _ru(m, bm) + g * bm
    n_row_tiles = mp // bm

    sizes = group_sizes.astype(_jnp.int32)
    starts = _jnp.concatenate([_jnp.zeros((1,), _jnp.int32), _jnp.cumsum(sizes)[:-1]])
    psizes = ((sizes + bm - 1) // bm) * bm                      # aligned sizes
    pstarts = _jnp.concatenate([_jnp.zeros((1,), _jnp.int32), _jnp.cumsum(psizes)[:-1]])

    # per PADDED row: which expert owns it, and which source row it is
    prow = _jnp.arange(mp, dtype=_jnp.int32)
    grp = _jnp.sum(prow[:, None] >= pstarts[None, :], axis=1) - 1   # [mp]
    grp = _jnp.clip(grp, 0, g - 1)
    off = prow - pstarts[grp]
    live = (off < sizes[grp]) & (prow < _jnp.sum(psizes))
    src = _jnp.where(live, _jnp.clip(starts[grp] + off, 0, m - 1), 0)

    lhs_p = _jnp.where(live[:, None], _jnp.take(lhs, src, axis=0), 0).astype(lhs.dtype)
    rhs_p = _jnp.pad(rhs, ((0, 0), (0, 0), (0, np_ - n))) if np_ != n else rhs
    # expert id of each ROW TILE: constant within a tile, by construction
    tile_grp = grp[::bm].astype(_jnp.int32)                     # [n_row_tiles]

    def _body(tg_ref, l_ref, r_ref, o_ref):
        gmm_tile(l_ref, r_ref, o_ref, bm=bm, bk=bk, bn=bn, k=k)

    out_p = _pl.pallas_call(
        _body,
        grid_spec=_pltpu.PrefetchScalarGridSpec(
            num_scalar_prefetch=1,
            grid=(n_row_tiles, np_ // bn),
            in_specs=[
                _pl.BlockSpec((bm, k), lambda i, j, tg: (i, 0)),          # lhs tile
                _pl.BlockSpec((1, k, bn), lambda i, j, tg: (tg[i], 0, j)),  # expert
            ],
            out_specs=_pl.BlockSpec((bm, bn), lambda i, j, tg: (i, j)),
        ),
        out_shape=_jax.ShapeDtypeStruct((mp, np_), _jnp.float32),
        compiler_params=_pltpu.CompilerParams(
            dimension_semantics=("arbitrary", "arbitrary"),
        ),
    )(tile_grp, lhs_p, rhs_p)

    # un-permute: original row r lives at padded row pstarts[grp_of_r] + (r - starts)
    row = _jnp.arange(m, dtype=_jnp.int32)
    rg = _jnp.sum(row[:, None] >= starts[None, :], axis=1) - 1
    rg = _jnp.clip(rg, 0, g - 1)
    dst = _jnp.clip(pstarts[rg] + (row - starts[rg]), 0, mp - 1)
    return _jnp.take(out_p[:, :n], dst, axis=0)
```

## What you must define

```python
def choose_tiles(m, k, n, num_groups):
    """All PYTHON INTS. returns (bm, bk, bn):
       bm : rows of `lhs` per grid step   -> rounded up to a multiple of 8
       bk : YOUR contraction chunk        -> rounded to a multiple of 128
       bn : output columns per grid step  -> rounded to a multiple of 128
    bm and bn shape the grid and the VMEM footprint; bk is entirely internal to
    gmm_tile and does not touch the BlockSpecs."""

def gmm_tile(l_ref, r_ref, o_ref, *, bm, bk, bn, k):
    """ONE grid step: one row tile x one column tile, for ONE expert.
       l_ref [bm, k]     bfloat16  this tile's rows of lhs, the FULL k axis
       r_ref [1, k, bn]  bfloat16  this expert's weights, the FULL k axis,
                                   columns j*bn .. j*bn+bn-1
       o_ref [bm, bn]    float32   THE OUTPUT. You must write it.
       bm, bk, bn, k : Python ints.
    Every row in this tile belongs to the SAME expert -- the harness guarantees
    it -- and rows past the expert's true size are already zeroed, so you never
    check a boundary. `pl.program_id(0)` is the row tile, `pl.program_id(1)` the
    column tile."""
```

## Where the real decisions are

* **`bm` and `bn` are the VMEM budget.** `l_ref` is `[bm, 4096]` bf16 and
  `r_ref` is `[1, 4096, bn]` bf16, and Pallas double-buffers both: at
  bm=512, bn=1024 that is 8 MB + 16 MB before the accumulator, against a ~32 MB
  scoped ceiling. Too big is `CompileTimeScopedVmemOom` at compile time; too
  small wastes the MXU. This is the main thing you are choosing.
* **`bm` also sets the padding waste.** The harness aligns every expert to a
  multiple of `bm`, so a large `bm` with many small experts pads a lot of dead
  rows -- and those dead rows are multiplied anyway.
* **The k loop.** `k=4096` is fully resident. One `[bm, 4096] x [4096, bn]`
  `dot_general`, or a loop over `bk`-wide chunks accumulating into an f32
  register. The MXU wants big contractions; the register file does not.
* **Accumulation dtype and matmul precision.** `preferred_element_type=
  jnp.float32` over bf16 operands is the standard grouped-matmul choice.
* **Loop order.** You control only what is inside one tile, but within it the
  order of the k chunks and where you keep the accumulator are yours.

## Task semantics

`out[rows of group g] = lhs[rows of group g] @ rhs[g]`, at fp32 accumulation.
`group_sizes` sums to `m` and **zero-size groups are legal and do occur** (the
harness handles them; you never see one). The fp32 oracle is
`jax.lax.ragged_dot(lhs.astype(f32), rhs.astype(f32), group_sizes)`.

## Declared shapes

Expert widths are the real ones: k=4096, n=14336, num_groups=4.

    m=4096, num_groups=4, k=4096, n=14336, uniform group sizes  (scored)
    m=2048, num_groups=4, k=4096, n=14336, Zipf-skewed          (scored)
    m=3000, num_groups=4, k=4096, n=14336, Zipf-skewed          (HOLDOUT:
        logged, not scored, but it MUST still trace -- 3000 is deliberately not
        a multiple of any reasonable row tile)

Group sizes are freshly sampled from BOTH uniform and Zipf-skewed
distributions on every grading, so a kernel tuned for one balance cannot fake
a win on the other.

## Judge contract

- Exec'd, then `jax.export`ed once per declared shape, no data, no device.
- MUST contain a real `pallas_call` -- the scaffold's counts.
- Correctness on hidden seeds against the fp32 oracle (max and q99 tail, 1.5x
  the reference's own bf16 error). Non-finite fails automatically.
- Determinism: 5 runs, BITWISE identical. Compile budget 90 s for all shapes.
- Score: median wall-clock against the tuned Pallas megablox `gmm` that MaxText
  vendors. Note that the harness's group-aligned permutation costs a gather and
  a scatter that the baseline does not pay, so this denominator is harsh.
- Banned: `jax.experimental.pallas.ops.*` (it holds the megablox kernel),
  `tpu_inference`, `MaxText`, `maxtext`, `skyrl`, `recurrentgemma`, the arena's
  package.

## Pallas dialect: the mistakes that actually killed candidates here

These are measured. Each line is the verbatim error and how many of the
previous attempts at these five kernels died on it.

1. **Names that DO NOT EXIST at this pin**, every one of them invented by a
   model on these exact tasks: `pl.load`, `pl.store`, `pl.swap`, `pl.Ref`,
   `pltpu.ANY`, `pltpu.TPUCompilerParams`, `jax.Shape`. A Ref is read and
   written by INDEXING, not by a load/store function. (The `pallas_call`
   keyword arguments that killed 4 more are the harness's problem here, not
   yours -- you do not write one.)

2. **Inside the kernel body you are handed Refs; a Ref is not an array.**
   Read `q_ref[...]`, `q_ref[0]`, `q_ref[:, :1]`; write `o_ref[...] = value`
   or `o_ref[0] = value`. A Ref has `.shape` and `.dtype` and NO `.astype` --
   read first, then cast.
3. **`pltpu.VMEM(shape, dtype)` is a SPECIFICATION, legal only inside
   `scratch_shapes=`.** Calling it in a kernel body returns a `MemoryRef`.
   Killed 9: `'MemoryRef' object does not support item assignment`. To
   accumulate inside a body use an ordinary value: `acc = jnp.zeros(...)`,
   `acc = acc + block`.
4. **A store into a Ref is not a broadcast** -- the right-hand side must
   already have the Ref's shape. Killed 6:
   `` Invalid shape for `swap`. Ref shape: (8, 4, 128) ... Value shape: () ``.
   Write `m_ref[...] = jnp.full(m_ref.shape, -1e30, m_ref.dtype)`, not
   `m_ref[...] = -1e30`.
5. **`dot_general`'s `dimension_numbers` is a PAIR OF PAIRS**, never flat.
   Killed 5 (`too many values to unpack (expected 2)`) plus several
   `dot_general requires ...`:
   `jax.lax.dot_general(x, y, (((1,), (1,)), ((), ())), preferred_element_type=jnp.float32)`
   -- that contracts axis 1 of x with axis 1 of y. WRONG: `((1,), (1,))`,
   `((1,), (1,), (0, 0))`.
6. **`@pl.when(cond)` is a DECORATOR that consumes the function and returns
   `None`.** Decorate and stop. Killed 2 (`'function' object is not iterable`,
   `'NoneType' object is not callable`). Never `with pl.when(...):`, never
   call the decorated name.
7. **Everything read out of a Ref is a TRACER.** `if`, `while`, `bool()`,
   `int()`, python `min`/`max` on one raise `TracerBoolConversionError`
   (killed 3). Use `jnp.where` for values and `@pl.when` for whole blocks.
   Python control flow on the STATIC ints (block sizes, page_size, head
   counts) is fine and unrolls at trace time.
8. **Mosaic tiling.** A block's last dim must be a multiple of 128 (or the
   whole dim) and its second-minor a multiple of 8 (or the whole dim). Killed
   1: `block shape are divisible by 8 and 128 respectively, or be equal to the
   respective dimensions of the overall array`. A `BlockSpec` block must also
   tile the operand: pad with `jnp.pad`, run the grid over the padded shape,
   slice the result back down. The HOLDOUT shape is in the declared set to
   force exactly this.
9. **`kernel` returns ONE array of the declared output shape and dtype**, not
   a tuple, not a Ref. Killed 2 (`function ... returned wrong type from jit`)
   and 2 more on layout (`cannot reshape array of shape (256, 4, 2560) into
   shape (4, 2560)`). If you scanned over time, `jnp.moveaxis` back before
   returning.
10. **You return VALUES, not Refs, and exactly the arity the scaffold
    unpacks.** Killed 5: `too many values to unpack (expected 2)`, and 3 more
    on `not enough values to unpack`. Read the scaffold above for what it does
    with your return.

One more, and unlike the rest it is not an API error -- it is the single
biggest cause of wrong ANSWERS here, so treat it as a rule.

**Keep every accumulator in float32. Pass `preferred_element_type=jnp.float32`
on every matmul.**

Your inputs are bfloat16 by contract. The MXU takes bfloat16 operands and sums
them in a wider float32 register -- but `jnp.dot`/`jnp.einsum` on two bfloat16
arrays RETURNS bfloat16, so that float32 accumulator is rounded away at the end
of every matmul unless you ask for it. bfloat16 carries about 3 decimal digits;
these kernels reduce over thousands of keys, pages, vocabulary entries or
timesteps, and the rounding compounds.

What a production TPU kernel does -- splash, vLLM-TPU, megablox, tokamax, all
of them -- is use bfloat16 ONLY as the matmul input format, because that is
what makes the MXU fast, and hold everything between matmuls at float32:
float32 logits, float32 softmax/LSE, float32 output accumulator. Narrow back to
bfloat16 only where a value is about to re-enter the MXU as an operand.

MEASURED against this judge's calibrated tolerance, across all five tasks: the
production path above lands at 0.4-0.7x of the budget, comfortably inside. The
same kernel written with the default dtypes -- bfloat16 logits, bfloat16
softmax, bfloat16 accumulator -- lands at 1.4x to 4.1x, i.e. it FAILS on
correctness while looking completely reasonable.

Do NOT reach for `precision=jax.lax.Precision.HIGHEST` instead. It is about 6x
the passes, no production kernel uses it for bfloat16 inputs, and buying
correctness with it just loses you the speed you are being scored on.

## Your current program

The complete program below was submitted to the judge. You may change ANY part
of it -- including the harness scaffolding, grid, and BlockSpecs -- as long as
it still defines `kernel(*inputs)` with the contract above.

```python
import jax
import jax.numpy as jnp

def choose_tiles(m, k, n, num_groups):
    """
    Returns (bm, bk, bn).
    bm: 512 allows high MXU utilization while remaining under the 32MB VMEM ceiling.
    bn: 512 maintains a balance between grid size and memory footprint.
    bk: 128 is provided as a standard contraction chunk, though the full k is resident.
    """
    return 512, 128, 512

def gmm_tile(l_ref, r_ref, o_ref, *, bm, bk, bn, k):
    """
    Performs one grid step: [bm, k] @ [k, bn] -> [bm, bn].
    
    l_ref: [bm, k] in bf16
    r_ref: [1, k, bn] in bf16
    o_ref: [bm, bn] in f32
    """
    # Read the resident tiles from MemoryRefs.
    # lhs: (bm, k)
    lhs = l_ref[...]
    
    # rhs: (k, bn)
    # The harness provides r_ref as [1, k, bn] to match the expert-prefeteched BlockSpec.
    rhs = r_ref[0, ...]
    
    # contract axis 1 of lhs (k) with axis 0 of rhs (k).
    # preferred_element_type=jnp.float32 ensures the accumulator is f32 for bf16 inputs.
    res = jax.lax.dot_general(
        lhs, 
        rhs, 
        (((1,), (0,)), ((), ())), 
        preferred_element_type=jnp.float32
    )
    
    # Store the f32 result back into the output Ref.
    o_ref[...] = res

# ===================== HARNESS SCAFFOLD -- appended verbatim =================
# Owns: the exclusive scan of group_sizes to row offsets, the group-ALIGNED row
# permutation (so no row tile ever straddles two experts -- the zero-size-group
# and boundary-tile correctness traps are the harness's, not yours), the
# pallas_call with scalar-prefetched per-tile expert ids, every BlockSpec, and
# the un-permute of the result.
import jax as _jax
import jax.numpy as _jnp
from jax.experimental import pallas as _pl
from jax.experimental.pallas import tpu as _pltpu


def _ru(x, m):
    return ((x + m - 1) // m) * m


def _tiles(m, k, n, g):
    bm, bk, bn = choose_tiles(int(m), int(k), int(n), int(g))
    bm = min(max(_ru(int(bm), 8), 8), 1024)
    bk = min(max(_ru(int(bk), 128), 128), int(_ru(k, 128)))
    bn = min(max(_ru(int(bn), 128), 128), 2048)
    return bm, bk, bn


def kernel(lhs, rhs, group_sizes):
    m, k = lhs.shape
    g, _k, n = rhs.shape
    bm, bk, bn = _tiles(m, k, n, g)
    np_ = _ru(n, bn)
    # static upper bound on the group-aligned row count: every expert wastes at
    # most bm-1 rows, and a zero-size expert wastes none.
    mp = _ru(m, bm) + g * bm
    n_row_tiles = mp // bm

    sizes = group_sizes.astype(_jnp.int32)
    starts = _jnp.concatenate([_jnp.zeros((1,), _jnp.int32), _jnp.cumsum(sizes)[:-1]])
    psizes = ((sizes + bm - 1) // bm) * bm                      # aligned sizes
    pstarts = _jnp.concatenate([_jnp.zeros((1,), _jnp.int32), _jnp.cumsum(psizes)[:-1]])

    # per PADDED row: which expert owns it, and which source row it is
    prow = _jnp.arange(mp, dtype=_jnp.int32)
    grp = _jnp.sum(prow[:, None] >= pstarts[None, :], axis=1) - 1   # [mp]
    grp = _jnp.clip(grp, 0, g - 1)
    off = prow - pstarts[grp]
    live = (off < sizes[grp]) & (prow < _jnp.sum(psizes))
    src = _jnp.where(live, _jnp.clip(starts[grp] + off, 0, m - 1), 0)

    lhs_p = _jnp.where(live[:, None], _jnp.take(lhs, src, axis=0), 0).astype(lhs.dtype)
    rhs_p = _jnp.pad(rhs, ((0, 0), (0, 0), (0, np_ - n))) if np_ != n else rhs
    # expert id of each ROW TILE: constant within a tile, by construction
    tile_grp = grp[::bm].astype(_jnp.int32)                     # [n_row_tiles]

    def _body(tg_ref, l_ref, r_ref, o_ref):
        gmm_tile(l_ref, r_ref, o_ref, bm=bm, bk=bk, bn=bn, k=k)

    out_p = _pl.pallas_call(
        _body,
        grid_spec=_pltpu.PrefetchScalarGridSpec(
            num_scalar_prefetch=1,
            grid=(n_row_tiles, np_ // bn),
            in_specs=[
                _pl.BlockSpec((bm, k), lambda i, j, tg: (i, 0)),          # lhs tile
                _pl.BlockSpec((1, k, bn), lambda i, j, tg: (tg[i], 0, j)),  # expert
            ],
            out_specs=_pl.BlockSpec((bm, bn), lambda i, j, tg: (i, j)),
        ),
        out_shape=_jax.ShapeDtypeStruct((mp, np_), _jnp.float32),
        compiler_params=_pltpu.CompilerParams(
            dimension_semantics=("arbitrary", "arbitrary"),
        ),
    )(tile_grp, lhs_p, rhs_p)

    # un-permute: original row r lives at padded row pstarts[grp_of_r] + (r - starts)
    row = _jnp.arange(m, dtype=_jnp.int32)
    rg = _jnp.sum(row[:, None] >= starts[None, :], axis=1) - 1
    rg = _jnp.clip(rg, 0, g - 1)
    dst = _jnp.clip(pstarts[rg] + (row - starts[rg]), 0, mp - 1)
    return _jnp.take(out_p[:, :n], dst, axis=0)

```

## Judge verdict

PASSED all correctness gates. Reward 0.589 -- your kernel runs at
59% of the speed of the production kernel on the same inputs
(reward = production_time / your_time; 1.0 means parity, above 1.0 beats it).
The remaining gap is structural, not a constant to tweak: consider the grid
and block sizes, VMEM working-set, online vs two-pass softmax, and whether
work can be skipped entirely (masked blocks, empty pages).

## Error signatures measured on previous attempts at these exact tasks

* Attention, max err ~0.05: your fully-masked rows are producing UNIFORM
  attention. With a finite mask value (-1e30) and a max-shifted softmax, a row
  whose every key is masked gets exp(0)=1 everywhere -- the mean of V, not 0.
  The contract requires exactly 0: keep a `row_live` mask and end with
  `out = jnp.where(row_live[..., None], out, 0.0)`.
* max err within ~1.5x of tol: precision, not logic. Keep every accumulator
  float32 -- `preferred_element_type=jnp.float32` on every matmul, float32
  running max/sum/output; bfloat16 only as matmul INPUTS.
* non-finite output (NaN/inf): mask-value arithmetic in the online-softmax
  rescale (inf - inf, or exp of a huge positive). Initialize the running max
  to a large FINITE negative and mask before exponentiating.
* O(1) error (max err > 1.0): a term is missing outright -- a dropped
  recurrence carry, a wrong head-group mapping. Re-derive the update rule
  against the task statement before touching anything else.

        *   Accumulator must be `f32`.
        *   `bm` and `bn` define the grid and VMEM.

        *   `bm = 512`, `bk = 128`, `bn = 512`.
        *   The `gmm_tile` function loads the whole `lhs` tile (`bm, k`) and the whole `rhs` tile (`k, bn`) into VMEM and performs a single `dot_general`.
        *   `lhs` size: $512 \times 4096 \times 2$ bytes $\approx 4.19$ MB.
        *   `rhs` size: $4096 \times 512 \times 2$ bytes $\approx 4.19$ MB.
        *   Output `o_ref` size: $512 \times 512 \times 4$ bytes $\approx 1.05$ MB.
        *   Pallas double-buffers inputs. So we have $2 \times (4.19 + 4.19) \approx 16.7$ MB.
        *   The memory usage is well under the 32MB limit, but maybe it's too conservative? Or maybe the MXU isn't being saturated because the blocks aren't optimal.

    *   The production kernel usually maximizes MXU utilization and minimizes memory movement.
    *   $k = 4096$. $n = 14336$.
    *   The grid is `(n_row_tiles, np_ // bn)`.
    *   `bm=512`, `bn=512` means the grid is roughly `(8 + 4, 14336 // 512) \approx (12, 28)`.
    *   The MXU works best with blocks that are larger, provided they fit in VMEM.
    *   Let's consider $bm=128, 256$ or $512$. $bn=128, 256, 512, 1024$.
    *   If we increase $bn$ to 1024:
        *   `lhs`: $512 \times 4096 \times 2 \approx 4$ MB.
        *   `rhs`: $4096 \times 1024 \times 2 \approx 8$ MB.
        *   `out`: $512 \times 1024 \times 4 \approx 2$ MB.
        *   Double buffering: $2 \times (4 + 8) = 24$ MB. Plus output. Still fits.
    *   What about $bm$? Larger $bm$ reduces the number of tiles (reducing grid overhead) but increases padding waste across experts. Since there are only 4 groups and $m$ is 4096, the padding waste is negligible (max $4 \times 512$ rows).
    *   Actually, the current implementation uses a *single* `dot_general` for the whole tile. On TPU, breaking the $k$ dimension into chunks (`bk`) can sometimes help with register pressure or allow different tiling strategies, but for a simple matmul, `dot_general` on the whole tile is usually the fastest way to hit the MXU.
    *   Wait, looking at the `gmm_tile` provided:
        ```python
        lhs = l_ref[...]
        rhs = r_ref[0, ...]
        res = jax.lax.dot_general(lhs, rhs, (((1,), (0,)), ((), ())), preferred_element_type=jnp.float32)
        o_ref[...] = res
        ```
        This is extremely clean. The bottleneck is likely the dimensions $bm$ and $bn$.
    *   TPU MXU dimensions are typically multiples of 128. $bm=512$ and $bn=512$ are good.
    *   Could $bm=128, bn=1024$ be better? Or $bm=256, bn=1024$?
    *   Generally, for the TPU, we want the "inner" dimension (the one that moves fastest) to be large.
    *   Let's try $bm=128, bn=1024$.
        *   `lhs` tile: $128 \times 4096 \times 2 \approx 1$ MB.
        *   `rhs` tile: $4096 \times 1024 \times 2 \approx 8$ MB.
        *   `out` tile: $128 \times 1024 \times 4 \approx 0.5$ MB.
        *   Total: $\sim 17$ MB.
    *   Actually, let's look at $bm=256, bn=1024$.
        *   `lhs`: 2 MB.
        *   `rhs`: 8 MB.
        *   `out`: 1 MB.
        *   Double buffer: $2 \times (2 + 8) = 20$ MB. + 1 MB out = 21 MB. Still safe.

    *   The production kernel (`megablox`) often uses a loop over $k$ using `bk` to keep the accumulator in registers and avoid writing/reading the output tile from VMEM until the end.
    *   Current code: `dot_general` does the whole $k$ at once. This is fine for a single tile.
    *   However, the Pallas `dot_general` is an abstraction. To really optimize, we might want to loop over $k$ manually if the compiler isn't doing it perfectly. But for $k=4096$, the whole `lhs` and `rhs` for the tile are already in VMEM (since the `BlockSpec` loads the full `k` dimension).
    *   Wait, look at the `BlockSpec` in the scaffold:
        `_pl.BlockSpec((bm, k), lambda i, j, tg: (i, 0))` -> This loads the ENTIRE $k$ axis.
        `_pl.BlockSpec((1, k, bn), lambda i, j, tg: (tg[i], 0, j))` -> This loads the ENTIRE $k$ axis for the weight expert.
    *   Since the entire $k$ axis is loaded into the tile's VMEM anyway, a single `dot_general` is effectively doing the loop.
    *   The performance gap is likely in the choice of `bm` and `bn`.
    *   For $k=4096, n=14336$, $bn$ should be as large as possible to minimize the number of times $lhs$ is loaded. If $bn=1024$, we load $lhs$ 14 times. If $bn=2048$, we load it 7 times.
    *   Let's try $bm=128, bn=1024$ or $bm=256, bn=1024$.
    *   Actually, let's try $bm=128$ to reduce padding waste (though not a major factor here) and maximize $bn$ to $1024$.

    *   Wait, if I use `bk` in a loop, I might be able to use smaller `BlockSpec`s? No, the `BlockSpec`s are in the *scaffold*, and I cannot change them.
    *   The scaffold's `BlockSpec`s are:
        `lhs`: `(bm, k)`
        `rhs`: `(1, k, bn)`
    *   This means the *hardware* will load the entire $k$ dimension into VMEM for every grid step.
    *   Given this, the only way to speed up is to make each grid step do as much work as possible.
    *   Let's maximize $bm$ and $bn$ within the VMEM limit.
    *   $bm=256, bn=1024$: $2 \times (256 \times 4096 \times 2 + 4096 \times 1024 \times 2) + (256 \times 1024 \times 4) = 2 \times (2 + 8) + 1 = 21$ MB.
    *   $bm=512, bn=1024$: $2 \times (512 \times 4096 \times 2 + 4096 \times 1024 \times 2) + (512 \times 1024 \times 4) = 2 \times (4 + 8) + 2 = 26$ MB.
    *   $bm=512, bn=2048$: $2 \times (4 + 16) + 4 = 44$ MB. (Too big, exceeds 32MB).
    *   $bm=256, bn=2048$: $2 \times (2 + 16) + 2 = 38$ MB. (Too big).
    *   $bm=128, bn=2048$: $2 \times (1 + 16) + 1 = 35$ MB. (Still likely too big).
    *   So $bm=512, bn=1024$ is likely the sweet spot for maximizing work per tile without OOMing.

    *   Does `jax.lax.dot_general` use the MXU efficiently? Yes.
    *   Is there any other overhead?
    *   The current `gmm_tile` loads the whole `l_ref[...]` and `r_ref[0, ...]`.
    *   One detail: `r_ref` is `(1, k, bn)`. `r_ref[0, ...]` creates a slice.
    *   Let's ensure the `dot_general` is as tight as possible.

    *   $m=4096$, $k=4096$, $n=14336$.
    *   $bm=512$, $bn=1024$.
    *   $n\_row\_tiles \approx (4096/512) + 4 \approx 12$.
    *   $n\_col\_tiles \approx 14336/1024 \approx 14$.
    *   Total tiles $\approx 168$.
    *   Each tile: $512 \times 4096 \times 1024$ operations.
    *   Total ops: $168 \times 512 \times 4096 \times 1024 \approx 3.6 \times 10^{11}$.
    *   Check: $m \times k \times n = 4096 \times 4096 \times 14336 \approx 2.4 \times 10^{11}$.
    *   The multiplication is proportional. The tiling is the main lever.

    *   Actually, another common TPU trick: if the output is `f32`, we should be careful about how we store it. Pallas `o_ref[...] = res` is correct.
    *   Wait, is there any advantage to using `bk`?
    *   If we use `bk`, we can keep the accumulator in registers. `dot_general` does this internally for a single call. However, manually looping over $k$ with `bk` and adding to an `f32` accumulator can sometimes be faster *if* the `dot_general` is not fusing well. But for this specific Pallas setup, `dot_general` is usually the way.
    *   Let's try $bm=512, bn=1024$.

    *   `bm=512`
    *   `bn=1024`
    *   `bk=128` (Internal, not really used if using one big `dot_general`, but I'll keep it for the signature).
    *   `LHS`: $512 \times 4096 \times 2 = 4,194,304$
    *   `RHS`: $4096 \times 1024 \times 2 = 8,388,608$
    *   `OUT`: $512 \times 1024 \times 4 = 2,097,152$
    *   Total per buffer: $14,680,064$
    *   Double buffered inputs: $2 \times (4.19 + 8.38) = 25.14$ MB.
    *   Output: $2.09$ MB.
    *   Total $\approx 27.23$ MB.
    *   This is very close to 32 MB but should fit.

    *   What if `bm=256, bn=1024`?
    *   Input buffers: $2 \times (2.09 + 8.38) = 20.94$ MB.
    *   Output: $1.04$ MB.
    *   Total $\approx 22$ MB. This is safer and might avoid any weird compiler issues with too much VMEM.

    *   Which is better? $bm=512$ or $bm=256$?
    *   Generally, TPU likes larger $bm$ and $bn$ as long as they are multiples of 128. $bm=512$ increases MXU occupancy.

    *   In the current `gmm_tile`, I'm using `lhs = l_ref[...]` and `rhs = r_ref[0, ...]`.
    *   This loads the whole buffer into a tracer.
    *   Is there a way to be faster?
    *   Using `jax.lax.dot_general` on the loaded arrays is the standard way to trigger the MXU.

    *   Let's double check the `dimension_numbers`.
    *   `lhs` shape: `(bm, k)`
    *   `rhs` shape: `(k, bn)`
    *   `dot_general` contracting axis 1 of `lhs` and axis 0 of `rhs`:
    *   `lhs` axes: 0 (bm), 1 (k)
    *   `rhs` axes: 0 (k), 1 (bn)
    *   `(((1,), (0,)), ((), ()))`
    *   First pair: (contracting axis of LHS, contracting axis of RHS).
    *   Second pair: (batch axis of LHS, batch axis of RHS).
    *   This is correct.

    *   One more thing: `preferred_element_type=jnp.float32` is critical. I have that.

    Let's consider $bm=256, bn=1024$. It's a very safe, high-performance configuration.
    Actually, $bm=512, bn=1024$ is better if it fits. Let's try $bm=128, bn=1024$ if we want to be even safer about padding? No, padding for 4 groups is tiny.
    Let's go with $bm=256, bn=1024$ to be safe and efficient.

    Wait, I just noticed the `_tiles` function in the scaffold:
    ```python
    def _tiles(m, k, n, g):
        bm, bk, bn = choose_tiles(int(m), int(k), int(n), int(g))
        bm = min(max(_ru(int(bm), 8), 8), 1024)
        bk = min(max(_ru(int(bk), 128), 128), int(_ru(k, 128)))
        bn = min(max(_ru(int(bn), 128), 128), 2048)
        return bm, bk, bn
    ```
    The scaffold *already* caps `bm` at 1024 and `bn` at 2048.
    If I return `bm=512, bn=1024`, the scaffold will keep them as is.

    Wait, the production kernel the user is being compared to likely uses a very optimized loop.
    Let's look at the `gmm_tile` again.
    ```python
    def gmm_tile(l_ref, r_ref, o_ref, *, bm, bk, bn, k):
        lhs = l_ref[...]
        rhs = r_ref[0, ...]
        res = jax.lax.dot_general(lhs, rhs, (((1,), (0,)), ((), ())), preferred_element_type=jnp.float32)
        o_ref[...] = res
    ```
    Is it possible that manually looping over `bk` is faster?
    If I loop over $k$ in chunks of `bk`, I can keep the accumulator in `f32` in registers.
    ```python
    acc = jnp.zeros((bm, bn), jnp.float32)
    for kk in range(0, k, bk):
        l_chunk = l_ref[:, kk:kk+bk]
        r_chunk = r_ref[0, kk:kk+bk, :]
        acc += jax.lax.dot_general(l_chunk, r_chunk, (((1,), (0,)), ((), ())), preferred_element_type=jnp.float32)
    o_ref[...] = acc
    ```
    This is a common pattern on TPU to reduce VMEM pressure on the accumulator and keep it in the register file (SRAM). However, the `lhs` and `rhs` tiles are *already* in VMEM because of the `BlockSpec`.
    The `dot_general` call internally handles the $k$ loop. If the whole tile is in VMEM, `dot_general` is effectively the same as the loop.
    The real difference is the tile size.

    Let's try:
    `bm = 256`
    `bn = 1024`

    Actually, let me rethink `bm`. If $m=4096$ and $k=4096$, $bm=256$ is a good divisor.```python
import jax
import jax.numpy as jnp

def choose_tiles(m, k, n, num_groups):
    """
    Tuning the VMEM footprint and MXU utilization.
    
    With k=4096, the inputs are large. 
    VMEM budget ~32MB. 
    bm=256, bn=1024 results in:
    - lhs tile: 256 * 4096 * 2 bytes = 2 MB
    - rhs tile: 4096 * 1024 * 2 bytes = 8 MB
    - output tile: 256 * 1024 * 4 bytes = 1 MB
    Double buffering (inputs): 2 * (2 + 8) = 20 MB.
    Total estimated: 21 MB.
    
    This maximizes the work per grid step (especially bn, reducing lhs loads) 
    while remaining safely below the VMEM ceiling to avoid OOM and allow 
    the compiler to optimize register allocation.
    """
    return 256, 128, 1024

def gmm_tile(l_ref, r_ref, o_ref, *, bm, bk, bn, k):
    """
    Performs a grouped matmul tile operation using the MXU.
    
    l_ref: [bm, k] bf16
    r_ref: [1, k, bn] bf16
    o_ref: [bm, bn] f32
    """
    # The harness provides the full k-dimension within the tile via BlockSpecs.
    # Load the resident tiles from MemoryRefs.
    lhs = l_ref[...]
    
    # rhs is [1, k, bn], we slice the first (and only) expert dimension.
    rhs = r_ref[0, ...]
    
    # Perform the contraction of axis 1 of lhs (k) and axis 0 of rhs (k).
    # preferred_element_type=jnp.float32 is essential: it forces the MXU 
    # to accumulate in f32 registers, preventing bfloat16 rounding 
    # errors over the 4096-long reduction.
    res = jax.lax.dot_general(
        lhs, 
        rhs, 
        (((1,), (0,)), ((), ())), 
        preferred_element_type=jnp.float32
    )
    
    # Store the resulting f32 accumulator back into the output Reference.
    o_ref[...] = res
```
Program submitted to the judge5,001 chars
import jax
import jax.numpy as jnp

def choose_tiles(m, k, n, num_groups):
    """
    Tuning the VMEM footprint and MXU utilization.
    
    With k=4096, the inputs are large. 
    VMEM budget ~32MB. 
    bm=256, bn=1024 results in:
    - lhs tile: 256 * 4096 * 2 bytes = 2 MB
    - rhs tile: 4096 * 1024 * 2 bytes = 8 MB
    - output tile: 256 * 1024 * 4 bytes = 1 MB
    Double buffering (inputs): 2 * (2 + 8) = 20 MB.
    Total estimated: 21 MB.
    
    This maximizes the work per grid step (especially bn, reducing lhs loads) 
    while remaining safely below the VMEM ceiling to avoid OOM and allow 
    the compiler to optimize register allocation.
    """
    return 256, 128, 1024

def gmm_tile(l_ref, r_ref, o_ref, *, bm, bk, bn, k):
    """
    Performs a grouped matmul tile operation using the MXU.
    
    l_ref: [bm, k] bf16
    r_ref: [1, k, bn] bf16
    o_ref: [bm, bn] f32
    """
    # The harness provides the full k-dimension within the tile via BlockSpecs.
    # Load the resident tiles from MemoryRefs.
    lhs = l_ref[...]
    
    # rhs is [1, k, bn], we slice the first (and only) expert dimension.
    rhs = r_ref[0, ...]
    
    # Perform the contraction of axis 1 of lhs (k) and axis 0 of rhs (k).
    # preferred_element_type=jnp.float32 is essential: it forces the MXU 
    # to accumulate in f32 registers, preventing bfloat16 rounding 
    # errors over the 4096-long reduction.
    res = jax.lax.dot_general(
        lhs, 
        rhs, 
        (((1,), (0,)), ((), ())), 
        preferred_element_type=jnp.float32
    )
    
    # Store the resulting f32 accumulator back into the output Reference.
    o_ref[...] = res

# ===================== HARNESS SCAFFOLD -- appended verbatim =================
# Owns: the exclusive scan of group_sizes to row offsets, the group-ALIGNED row
# permutation (so no row tile ever straddles two experts -- the zero-size-group
# and boundary-tile correctness traps are the harness's, not yours), the
# pallas_call with scalar-prefetched per-tile expert ids, every BlockSpec, and
# the un-permute of the result.
import jax as _jax
import jax.numpy as _jnp
from jax.experimental import pallas as _pl
from jax.experimental.pallas import tpu as _pltpu


def _ru(x, m):
    return ((x + m - 1) // m) * m


def _tiles(m, k, n, g):
    bm, bk, bn = choose_tiles(int(m), int(k), int(n), int(g))
    bm = min(max(_ru(int(bm), 8), 8), 1024)
    bk = min(max(_ru(int(bk), 128), 128), int(_ru(k, 128)))
    bn = min(max(_ru(int(bn), 128), 128), 2048)
    return bm, bk, bn


def kernel(lhs, rhs, group_sizes):
    m, k = lhs.shape
    g, _k, n = rhs.shape
    bm, bk, bn = _tiles(m, k, n, g)
    np_ = _ru(n, bn)
    # static upper bound on the group-aligned row count: every expert wastes at
    # most bm-1 rows, and a zero-size expert wastes none.
    mp = _ru(m, bm) + g * bm
    n_row_tiles = mp // bm

    sizes = group_sizes.astype(_jnp.int32)
    starts = _jnp.concatenate([_jnp.zeros((1,), _jnp.int32), _jnp.cumsum(sizes)[:-1]])
    psizes = ((sizes + bm - 1) // bm) * bm                      # aligned sizes
    pstarts = _jnp.concatenate([_jnp.zeros((1,), _jnp.int32), _jnp.cumsum(psizes)[:-1]])

    # per PADDED row: which expert owns it, and which source row it is
    prow = _jnp.arange(mp, dtype=_jnp.int32)
    grp = _jnp.sum(prow[:, None] >= pstarts[None, :], axis=1) - 1   # [mp]
    grp = _jnp.clip(grp, 0, g - 1)
    off = prow - pstarts[grp]
    live = (off < sizes[grp]) & (prow < _jnp.sum(psizes))
    src = _jnp.where(live, _jnp.clip(starts[grp] + off, 0, m - 1), 0)

    lhs_p = _jnp.where(live[:, None], _jnp.take(lhs, src, axis=0), 0).astype(lhs.dtype)
    rhs_p = _jnp.pad(rhs, ((0, 0), (0, 0), (0, np_ - n))) if np_ != n else rhs
    # expert id of each ROW TILE: constant within a tile, by construction
    tile_grp = grp[::bm].astype(_jnp.int32)                     # [n_row_tiles]

    def _body(tg_ref, l_ref, r_ref, o_ref):
        gmm_tile(l_ref, r_ref, o_ref, bm=bm, bk=bk, bn=bn, k=k)

    out_p = _pl.pallas_call(
        _body,
        grid_spec=_pltpu.PrefetchScalarGridSpec(
            num_scalar_prefetch=1,
            grid=(n_row_tiles, np_ // bn),
            in_specs=[
                _pl.BlockSpec((bm, k), lambda i, j, tg: (i, 0)),          # lhs tile
                _pl.BlockSpec((1, k, bn), lambda i, j, tg: (tg[i], 0, j)),  # expert
            ],
            out_specs=_pl.BlockSpec((bm, bn), lambda i, j, tg: (i, j)),
        ),
        out_shape=_jax.ShapeDtypeStruct((mp, np_), _jnp.float32),
        compiler_params=_pltpu.CompilerParams(
            dimension_semantics=("arbitrary", "arbitrary"),
        ),
    )(tile_grp, lhs_p, rhs_p)

    # un-permute: original row r lives at padded row pstarts[grp_of_r] + (r - starts)
    row = _jnp.arange(m, dtype=_jnp.int32)
    rg = _jnp.sum(row[:, None] >= starts[None, :], axis=1) - 1
    rg = _jnp.clip(rg, 0, g - 1)
    dst = _jnp.clip(pstarts[rg] + (row - starts[rg]), 0, mp - 1)
    return _jnp.take(out_p[:, :n], dst, axis=0)

# Owns: the exclusive scan of group_sizes to row offsets, the group-ALIGNED row
# permutation (so no row tile ever straddles two experts -- the zero-size-group
# and boundary-tile correctness traps are the harness's, not yours), the
# pallas_call with scalar-prefetched per-tile expert ids, every BlockSpec, and

import jax as _jax
import jax.numpy as _jnp
from jax.experimental import pallas as _pl
from jax.experimental.pallas import tpu as _pltpu


def _ru(x, m):
    return ((x + m - 1) // m) * m


def _tiles(m, k, n, g):
    bm, bk, bn = choose_tiles(int(m), int(k), int(n), int(g))
    bm = min(max(_ru(int(bm), 8), 8), 1024)
    bk = min(max(_ru(int(bk), 128), 128), int(_ru(k, 128)))
    bn = min(max(_ru(int(bn), 128), 128), 2048)
    return bm, bk, bn


def kernel(lhs, rhs, group_sizes):
    m, k = lhs.shape
    g, _k, n = rhs.shape
    bm, bk, bn = _tiles(m, k, n, g)
    np_ = _ru(n, bn)
    # static upper bound on the group-aligned row count: every expert wastes at
    # most bm-1 rows, and a zero-size expert wastes none.

    n_row_tiles = mp // bm

    psizes = ((sizes + bm - 1) // bm) * bm                      # aligned sizes
    pstarts = _jnp.concatenate([_jnp.zeros((1,), _jnp.int32), _jnp.cumsum(psizes)[:-1]])

    # per PADDED row: which expert owns it, and which source row it is
    prow = _jnp.arange(mp, dtype=_jnp.int32)
    grp = _jnp.sum(prow[:, None] >= pstarts[None, :], axis=1) - 1   # [mp]
    grp = _jnp.clip(grp, 0, g - 1)
    off = prow - pstarts[grp]
    live = (off < sizes[grp]) & (prow < _jnp.sum(psizes))
    src = _jnp.where(live, _jnp.clip(starts[grp] + off, 0, m - 1), 0)

    lhs_p = _jnp.where(live[:, None], _jnp.take(lhs, src, axis=0), 0).astype(lhs.dtype)
    rhs_p = _jnp.pad(rhs, ((0, 0), (0, 0), (0, np_ - n))) if np_ != n else rhs
    # expert id of each ROW TILE: constant within a tile, by construction
    tile_grp = grp[::bm].astype(_jnp.int32)                     # [n_row_tiles]

    def _body(tg_ref, l_ref, r_ref, o_ref):
        gmm_tile(l_ref, r_ref, o_ref, bm=bm, bk=bk, bn=bn, k=k)

    out_p = _pl.pallas_call(
        _body,
        grid_spec=_pltpu.PrefetchScalarGridSpec(
            num_scalar_prefetch=1,
            grid=(n_row_tiles, np_ // bn),
            in_specs=[
                _pl.BlockSpec((bm, k), lambda i, j, tg: (i, 0)),          # lhs tile
                _pl.BlockSpec((1, k, bn), lambda i, j, tg: (tg[i], 0, j)),  # expert

            out_specs=_pl.BlockSpec((bm, bn), lambda i, j, tg: (i, j)),

        compiler_params=_pltpu.CompilerParams(
            dimension_semantics=("arbitrary", "arbitrary"),

    row = _jnp.arange(m, dtype=_jnp.int32)

## What you must define

```python
def choose_tiles(m, k, n, num_groups):
    """All PYTHON INTS. returns (bm, bk, bn):
       bm : rows of `lhs` per grid step   -> rounded up to a multiple of 8
       bk : YOUR contraction chunk        -> rounded to a multiple of 128
       bn : output columns per grid step  -> rounded to a multiple of 128
    bm and bn shape the grid and the VMEM footprint; bk is entirely internal to
    gmm_tile and does not touch the BlockSpecs."""

def gmm_tile(l_ref, r_ref, o_ref, *, bm, bk, bn, k):
    """ONE grid step: one row tile x one column tile, for ONE expert.
       l_ref [bm, k]     bfloat16  this tile's rows of lhs, the FULL k axis
       r_ref [1, k, bn]  bfloat16  this expert's weights, the FULL k axis,
                                   columns j*bn .. j*bn+bn-1
       o_ref [bm, bn]    float32   THE OUTPUT. You must write it.
       bm, bk, bn, k : Python ints.
    Every row in this tile belongs to the SAME expert -- the harness guarantees
    it -- and rows past the expert's true size are already zeroed, so you never
    check a boundary. `pl.program_id(0)` is the row tile, `pl.program_id(1)` the
    column tile."""
```

## Where the real decisions are

* **`bm` and `bn` are the VMEM budget.** `l_ref` is `[bm, 4096]` bf16 and
  `r_ref` is `[1, 4096, bn]` bf16, and Pallas double-buffers both: at
  bm=512, bn=1024 that is 8 MB + 16 MB before the accumulator, against a ~32 MB
  scoped ceiling. Too big is `CompileTimeScopedVmemOom` at compile time; too
  small wastes the MXU. This is the main thing you are choosing.
* **`bm` also sets the padding waste.** The harness aligns every expert to a
  multiple of `bm`, so a large `bm` with many small experts pads a lot of dead
  rows -- and those dead rows are multiplied anyway.
* **The k loop.** `k=4096` is fully resident. One `[bm, 4096] x [4096, bn]`
  `dot_general`, or a loop over `bk`-wide chunks accumulating into an f32
  register. The MXU wants big contractions; the register file does not.
* **Accumulation dtype and matmul precision.** `preferred_element_type=
  jnp.float32` over bf16 operands is the standard grouped-matmul choice.
* **Loop order.** You control only what is inside one tile, but within it the
  order of the k chunks and where you keep the accumulator are yours.

## Task semantics

`out[rows of group g] = lhs[rows of group g] @ rhs[g]`, at fp32 accumulation.
`group_sizes` sums to `m` and **zero-size groups are legal and do occur** (the
harness handles them; you never see one). The fp32 oracle is
`jax.lax.ragged_dot(lhs.astype(f32), rhs.astype(f32), group_sizes)`.

## Declared shapes

Expert widths are the real ones: k=4096, n=14336, num_groups=4.

    m=4096, num_groups=4, k=4096, n=14336, uniform group sizes  (scored)
    m=2048, num_groups=4, k=4096, n=14336, Zipf-skewed          (scored)
    m=3000, num_groups=4, k=4096, n=14336, Zipf-skewed          (HOLDOUT:
        logged, not scored, but it MUST still trace -- 3000 is deliberately not
        a multiple of any reasonable row tile)

Group sizes are freshly sampled from BOTH uniform and Zipf-skewed
distributions on every grading, so a kernel tuned for one balance cannot fake
a win on the other.

## Judge contract

- Exec'd, then `jax.export`ed once per declared shape, no data, no device.
- MUST contain a real `pallas_call` -- the scaffold's counts.
- Correctness on hidden seeds against the fp32 oracle (max and q99 tail, 1.5x
  the reference's own bf16 error). Non-finite fails automatically.
- Determinism: 5 runs, BITWISE identical. Compile budget 90 s for all shapes.
- Score: median wall-clock against the tuned Pallas megablox `gmm` that MaxText
  vendors. Note that the harness's group-aligned permutation costs a gather and
  a scatter that the baseline does not pay, so this denominator is harsh.
- Banned: `jax.experimental.pallas.ops.*` (it holds the megablox kernel),
  `tpu_inference`, `MaxText`, `maxtext`, `skyrl`, `recurrentgemma`, the arena's
  package.

## Pallas dialect: the mistakes that actually killed candidates here

These are measured. Each line is the verbatim error and how many of the
previous attempts at these five kernels died on it.

1. **Names that DO NOT EXIST at this pin**, every one of them invented by a
   model on these exact tasks: `pl.load`, `pl.store`, `pl.swap`, `pl.Ref`,
   `pltpu.ANY`, `pltpu.TPUCompilerParams`, `jax.Shape`. A Ref is read and
   written by INDEXING, not by a load/store function. (The `pallas_call`
   keyword arguments that killed 4 more are the harness's problem here, not
   yours -- you do not write one.)

2. **Inside the kernel body you are handed Refs; a Ref is not an array.**
   Read `q_ref[...]`, `q_ref[0]`, `q_ref[:, :1]`; write `o_ref[...] = value`
   or `o_ref[0] = value`. A Ref has `.shape` and `.dtype` and NO `.astype` --
   read first, then cast.
3. **`pltpu.VMEM(shape, dtype)` is a SPECIFICATION, legal only inside
   `scratch_shapes=`.** Calling it in a kernel body returns a `MemoryRef`.
   Killed 9: `'MemoryRef' object does not support item assignment`. To
   accumulate inside a body use an ordinary value: `acc = jnp.zeros(...)`,
   `acc = acc + block`.
4. **A store into a Ref is not a broadcast** -- the right-hand side must
   already have the Ref's shape. Killed 6:
   `` Invalid shape for `swap`. Ref shape: (8, 4, 128) ... Value shape: () ``.
   Write `m_ref[...] = jnp.full(m_ref.shape, -1e30, m_ref.dtype)`, not
   `m_ref[...] = -1e30`.
5. **`dot_general`'s `dimension_numbers` is a PAIR OF PAIRS**, never flat.
   Killed 5 (`too many values to unpack (expected 2)`) plus several
   `dot_general requires ...`:
   `jax.lax.dot_general(x, y, (((1,), (1,)), ((), ())), preferred_element_type=jnp.float32)`
   -- that contracts axis 1 of x with axis 1 of y. WRONG: `((1,), (1,))`,
   `((1,), (1,), (0, 0))`.
6. **`@pl.when(cond)` is a DECORATOR that consumes the function and returns
   `None`.** Decorate and stop. Killed 2 (`'function' object is not iterable`,
   `'NoneType' object is not callable`). Never `with pl.when(...):`, never
   call the decorated name.
7. **Everything read out of a Ref is a TRACER.** `if`, `while`, `bool()`,
   `int()`, python `min`/`max` on one raise `TracerBoolConversionError`
   (killed 3). Use `jnp.where` for values and `@pl.when` for whole blocks.
   Python control flow on the STATIC ints (block sizes, page_size, head
   counts) is fine and unrolls at trace time.
8. **Mosaic tiling.** A block's last dim must be a multiple of 128 (or the
   whole dim) and its second-minor a multiple of 8 (or the whole dim). Killed
   1: `block shape are divisible by 8 and 128 respectively, or be equal to the
   respective dimensions of the overall array`. A `BlockSpec` block must also
   tile the operand: pad with `jnp.pad`, run the grid over the padded shape,
   slice the result back down. The HOLDOUT shape is in the declared set to
   force exactly this.
9. **`kernel` returns ONE array of the declared output shape and dtype**, not
   a tuple, not a Ref. Killed 2 (`function ... returned wrong type from jit`)
   and 2 more on layout (`cannot reshape array of shape (256, 4, 2560) into
   shape (4, 2560)`). If you scanned over time, `jnp.moveaxis` back before
   returning.
10. **You return VALUES, not Refs, and exactly the arity the scaffold
    unpacks.** Killed 5: `too many values to unpack (expected 2)`, and 3 more
    on `not enough values to unpack`. Read the scaffold above for what it does
    with your return.

One more, and unlike the rest it is not an API error -- it is the single
biggest cause of wrong ANSWERS here, so treat it as a rule.

**Keep every accumulator in float32. Pass `preferred_element_type=jnp.float32`
on every matmul.**

Your inputs are bfloat16 by contract. The MXU takes bfloat16 operands and sums
them in a wider float32 register -- but `jnp.dot`/`jnp.einsum` on two bfloat16
arrays RETURNS bfloat16, so that float32 accumulator is rounded away at the end
of every matmul unless you ask for it. bfloat16 carries about 3 decimal digits;
these kernels reduce over thousands of keys, pages, vocabulary entries or
timesteps, and the rounding compounds.

What a production TPU kernel does -- splash, vLLM-TPU, megablox, tokamax, all
of them -- is use bfloat16 ONLY as the matmul input format, because that is
what makes the MXU fast, and hold everything between matmuls at float32:
float32 logits, float32 softmax/LSE, float32 output accumulator. Narrow back to
bfloat16 only where a value is about to re-enter the MXU as an operand.

MEASURED against this judge's calibrated tolerance, across all five tasks: the
production path above lands at 0.4-0.7x of the budget, comfortably inside. The
same kernel written with the default dtypes -- bfloat16 logits, bfloat16
softmax, bfloat16 accumulator -- lands at 1.4x to 4.1x, i.e. it FAILS on
correctness while looking completely reasonable.

Do NOT reach for `precision=jax.lax.Precision.HIGHEST` instead. It is about 6x
the passes, no production kernel uses it for bfloat16 inputs, and buying
correctness with it just loses you the speed you are being scored on.

## Your current program

The complete program below was submitted to the judge. You may change ANY part
of it -- including the harness scaffolding, grid, and BlockSpecs -- as long as
it still defines `kernel(*inputs)` with the contract above.

```python
import jax
import jax.numpy as jnp

def choose_tiles(m, k, n, num_groups):
    """
    Tuning the VMEM footprint and MXU utilization.
    
    With k=4096, the inputs are large. 
    VMEM budget ~32MB. 
    bm=256, bn=1024 results in:
    - lhs tile: 256 * 4096 * 2 bytes = 2 MB
    - rhs tile: 4096 * 1024 * 2 bytes = 8 MB
    - output tile: 256 * 1024 * 4 bytes = 1 MB
    Double buffering (inputs): 2 * (2 + 8) = 20 MB.
    Total estimated: 21 MB.
    
    This maximizes the work per grid step (especially bn, reducing lhs loads) 
    while remaining safely below the VMEM ceiling to avoid OOM and allow 
    the compiler to optimize register allocation.
    """
    return 256, 128, 1024

def gmm_tile(l_ref, r_ref, o_ref, *, bm, bk, bn, k):
    """
    Performs a grouped matmul tile operation using the MXU.
    
    l_ref: [bm, k] bf16
    r_ref: [1, k, bn] bf16
    o_ref: [bm, bn] f32
    """
    # The harness provides the full k-dimension within the tile via BlockSpecs.
    # Load the resident tiles from MemoryRefs.
    lhs = l_ref[...]
    
    # rhs is [1, k, bn], we slice the first (and only) expert dimension.
    rhs = r_ref[0, ...]
    
    # Perform the contraction of axis 1 of lhs (k) and axis 0 of rhs (k).
    # preferred_element_type=jnp.float32 is essential: it forces the MXU 
    # to accumulate in f32 registers, preventing bfloat16 rounding 
    # errors over the 4096-long reduction.
    res = jax.lax.dot_general(
        lhs, 
        rhs, 
        (((1,), (0,)), ((), ())), 
        preferred_element_type=jnp.float32
    )
    
    # Store the resulting f32 accumulator back into the output Reference.
    o_ref[...] = res

# ===================== HARNESS SCAFFOLD -- appended verbatim =================
# Owns: the exclusive scan of group_sizes to row offsets, the group-ALIGNED row
# permutation (so no row tile ever straddles two experts -- the zero-size-group
# and boundary-tile correctness traps are the harness's, not yours), the
# pallas_call with scalar-prefetched per-tile expert ids, every BlockSpec, and
# the un-permute of the result.
import jax as _jax
import jax.numpy as _jnp
from jax.experimental import pallas as _pl
from jax.experimental.pallas import tpu as _pltpu


def _ru(x, m):
    return ((x + m - 1) // m) * m


def _tiles(m, k, n, g):
    bm, bk, bn = choose_tiles(int(m), int(k), int(n), int(g))
    bm = min(max(_ru(int(bm), 8), 8), 1024)
    bk = min(max(_ru(int(bk), 128), 128), int(_ru(k, 128)))
    bn = min(max(_ru(int(bn), 128), 128), 2048)
    return bm, bk, bn


def kernel(lhs, rhs, group_sizes):
    m, k = lhs.shape
    g, _k, n = rhs.shape
    bm, bk, bn = _tiles(m, k, n, g)
    np_ = _ru(n, bn)
    # static upper bound on the group-aligned row count: every expert wastes at
    # most bm-1 rows, and a zero-size expert wastes none.
    mp = _ru(m, bm) + g * bm
    n_row_tiles = mp // bm

    sizes = group_sizes.astype(_jnp.int32)
    starts = _jnp.concatenate([_jnp.zeros((1,), _jnp.int32), _jnp.cumsum(sizes)[:-1]])
    psizes = ((sizes + bm - 1) // bm) * bm                      # aligned sizes
    pstarts = _jnp.concatenate([_jnp.zeros((1,), _jnp.int32), _jnp.cumsum(psizes)[:-1]])

    # per PADDED row: which expert owns it, and which source row it is
    prow = _jnp.arange(mp, dtype=_jnp.int32)
    grp = _jnp.sum(prow[:, None] >= pstarts[None, :], axis=1) - 1   # [mp]
    grp = _jnp.clip(grp, 0, g - 1)
    off = prow - pstarts[grp]
    live = (off < sizes[grp]) & (prow < _jnp.sum(psizes))
    src = _jnp.where(live, _jnp.clip(starts[grp] + off, 0, m - 1), 0)

    lhs_p = _jnp.where(live[:, None], _jnp.take(lhs, src, axis=0), 0).astype(lhs.dtype)
    rhs_p = _jnp.pad(rhs, ((0, 0), (0, 0), (0, np_ - n))) if np_ != n else rhs
    # expert id of each ROW TILE: constant within a tile, by construction
    tile_grp = grp[::bm].astype(_jnp.int32)                     # [n_row_tiles]

    def _body(tg_ref, l_ref, r_ref, o_ref):
        gmm_tile(l_ref, r_ref, o_ref, bm=bm, bk=bk, bn=bn, k=k)

    out_p = _pl.pallas_call(
        _body,
        grid_spec=_pltpu.PrefetchScalarGridSpec(
            num_scalar_prefetch=1,
            grid=(n_row_tiles, np_ // bn),
            in_specs=[
                _pl.BlockSpec((bm, k), lambda i, j, tg: (i, 0)),          # lhs tile
                _pl.BlockSpec((1, k, bn), lambda i, j, tg: (tg[i], 0, j)),  # expert
            ],
            out_specs=_pl.BlockSpec((bm, bn), lambda i, j, tg: (i, j)),
        ),
        out_shape=_jax.ShapeDtypeStruct((mp, np_), _jnp.float32),
        compiler_params=_pltpu.CompilerParams(
            dimension_semantics=("arbitrary", "arbitrary"),
        ),
    )(tile_grp, lhs_p, rhs_p)

    # un-permute: original row r lives at padded row pstarts[grp_of_r] + (r - starts)
    row = _jnp.arange(m, dtype=_jnp.int32)
    rg = _jnp.sum(row[:, None] >= starts[None, :], axis=1) - 1
    rg = _jnp.clip(rg, 0, g - 1)
    dst = _jnp.clip(pstarts[rg] + (row - starts[rg]), 0, mp - 1)
    return _jnp.take(out_p[:, :n], dst, axis=0)

```

## Judge verdict

PASSED all correctness gates. Reward 0.513 -- your kernel runs at
51% of the speed of the production kernel on the same inputs
(reward = production_time / your_time; 1.0 means parity, above 1.0 beats it).
The remaining gap is structural, not a constant to tweak: consider the grid
and block sizes, VMEM working-set, online vs two-pass softmax, and whether
work can be skipped entirely (masked blocks, empty pages).

## Error signatures measured on previous attempts at these exact tasks

* Attention, max err ~0.05: your fully-masked rows are producing UNIFORM
  attention. With a finite mask value (-1e30) and a max-shifted softmax, a row
  whose every key is masked gets exp(0)=1 everywhere -- the mean of V, not 0.
  The contract requires exactly 0: keep a `row_live` mask and end with
  `out = jnp.where(row_live[..., None], out, 0.0)`.
* max err within ~1.5x of tol: precision, not logic. Keep every accumulator
  float32 -- `preferred_element_type=jnp.float32` on every matmul, float32
  running max/sum/output; bfloat16 only as matmul INPUTS.
* non-finite output (NaN/inf): mask-value arithmetic in the online-softmax
  rescale (inf - inf, or exp of a huge positive). Initialize the running max
  to a large FINITE negative and mask before exponentiating.
* O(1) error (max err > 1.0): a term is missing outright -- a dropped
  recurrence carry, a wrong head-group mapping. Re-derive the update rule
  against the task statement before touching anything else.

    *   $bm$ and $bn$ are the primary levers for VMEM.
    *   $bm=256, bn=1024$ was 51%.
    *   Let's try a slightly larger $bm$ and smaller $bn$ to see if it balances better?
    *   Or maybe $bm=128, bn=1024$? (Less padding waste).
    *   Actually, $k$ is the biggest dimension. The-most-efficient-TPU-way is usually to have the "inner" loop dimension (the one that stays in registers) be the one that's largest.
    *   Since $k=4096$ is quite large, maybe we *should* loop over it inside the kernel to avoid one massive `dot_general` that might be spilling registers?

    *   Current total VMEM: $\sim 21\text{ MB}$.
    *   $bm=256, bn=1024, k=4096$.
    *   Let's try $bm=128, bn=1024$ to reduce padding and see if we can fit a loop.
    *   Wait, if I use a loop, I can use a smaller `bk`. That doesn't change the VMEM used by the `Ref`s (because the `BlockSpec` is fixed), but it changes how the hardware executes.

    *   Let's refine the loop approach:
        ```python
        def gmm_tile(l_ref, r_ref, o_ref, *, bm, bk, bn, k):
            acc = jnp.zeros((bm, bn), jnp.float32)
            for kk in range(0, k, bk):
                # l_ref is [bm, k]
                # r_ref is [1, k, bn]
                l_chunk = l_ref[:, kk : kk + bk]
                r_chunk = r_ref[0, kk : kk + bk, :]
                acc += jax.lax.dot_general(
                    l_chunk, r_chunk, (((1,), (0,)), ((), ())),
                    preferred_element_type=jnp.float32
                )
            o_ref[...] = acc
        ```
    *   Wait, does indexing `l_ref[:, kk : kk + bk]` create a copy or a view? In Pallas, indexing into a `MemoryRef` returns a value (a tracer).
    *   This loop is the standard way to implement high-performance kernels.

    *   Let's try $bm=128$ (to be safer with padding and VMEM) and $bn=1024$.
    *   $k=4096$, $bk=128$ (standard).
    *   Will $bm=128$ be too small for the MXU? The MXU's native block is $128 \times 128$. $128$ is exactly one block. $256$ is two. Both are fine.
    *   Let's try $bm=256, bn=1024, bk=128$.

    *   One detail: The `dot_general` call inside a loop.
    *   The current implementation does `l_ref[...]` (loads all) then `dot_general`.
    *   The loop implementation: `l_ref[:, kk : kk + bk]` (loads chunk).
    *   This is much more likely to be what the "production kernel" does because it allows the compiler to pipeline.

    *   Wait, the prompt says: "The gap is structural... consider... whether work can be skipped entirely (masked blocks, empty pages)".
    *   How can I skip work without changing the scaffold?
    *   I can check if the `l_chunk` is all zeros. But that's probably slower.
    *   Is there any other way?
    *   Wait! I can check if the entire `l_ref` is zero *once* at the start of the kernel.
    *   `if jnp.all(l_ref[...] == 0): return`. But `if` is banned on tracers.
    *   I must use `@pl.when`.
    *   But `@pl.when` requires a condition. I can check `jnp.any(l_ref[...] != 0)`.
    *   However, the scaffold's `_body` is the one that calls `gmm_tile`. If I want to use `@pl.when`, I'd have to put it inside `gmm_tile`.

    *   Let's try:
        ```python
        def gmm_tile(l_ref, r_ref, o_ref, *, bm, bk, bn, k):
            # We can't use python 'if' on tracers.
            # We can use jnp.where or pl.when.
            # But pl.when is a decorator.
            
            # Try the loop first. It's the most standard structural fix.
            ...
        ```

    *   Actually, looking at the VMEM again. $bm=256, bn=1024, k=4096$ uses $\sim 21\text{MB}$.
    *   If I increase $bn$ to $2048$, I move to $40\text{MB}$ (too high).
    *   If I increase $bm$ to $512$, I move to $28\text{MB}$ (possible).
    *   Let's try $bm=512, bn=1024, bk=128$ with the loop.

    *   Wait, one more thing. The `preferred_element_type=jnp.float32` only works if the *output* of `dot_general` is that type.
    *   In the loop: `acc += dot_general(..., preferred_element_type=jnp.float32)`.
    *   Since `acc` is `float32`, this should all be in `float32`.

    *   imports: `jax, jax.numpy as jnp`.
    *   `choose_tiles`: return `(512, 128, 1024)`.
    *   `gmm_tile`: implement loop over $bk$.
    *   Ensure `o_ref[...] = acc` is the final step.

    *   $bm=512, bk=128, bn=1024$.
    *   `l_ref` [512, 4096] bf16 $\approx 4\text{MB}$.
    *   `r_ref` [1, 4096, 1024] bf16 $\approx 8\text{MB}$.
    *   `o_ref` [512, 1024] f32 $\approx 2\text{MB}$.
    *   Sum = $14\text{MB}$.
    *   Pallas double-buffers inputs: $2 \times (4 + 8) = 24\text{MB}$.
    *   $24 + 2 = 26\text{MB}$.
    *   This is under $32\text{MB}$.

    *   A single `dot_general` is very efficient on TPUs, but if the $k$ dimension is huge, the Pallas compiler can sometimes struggle with the VMEM $\rightarrow$ MXU mapping if it's one giant operation. Slicing it into $bk$ chunks often allows for better software pipelining (loading the next chunk while computing the current one).

    *   If `l_ref` contains a row that is entirely padding, that row's output is 0.
    *   The harness pads experts to multiples of `bm`.
    *   Inside `gmm_tile`, `l_ref` is $[bm, k]$. Some of these $bm$ rows might be the padding rows.
    *   Is there a way to skip them? Not easily without the `group_sizes` or the indices.
    *   But wait, if we use `dot_general`, the MXU will just multiply zeros by the weights. That's very fast. The "structural gap" might just be the $bm/bn$ choice and the pipelining.

    *   $bm=512$ is $2 \times 256$. It doubles the MXU usage per grid step.
    *   $bn=1024$ is quite large.
    *   Let's go with $bm=512, bn=1024, bk=128$.

    *   `pl.ds` vs slicing?
    *   `l_ref[:, kk:kk+bk]` is equivalent to `pl.ds(l_ref, (0, kk), (bm, bk))`. Slicing is cleaner.```python
import jax
import jax.numpy as jnp

def choose_tiles(m, k, n, num_groups):
    """
    Choosing bm=512 and bn=1024 to maximize MXU utilization while staying 
    under the ~32MB VMEM ceiling.
    
    VMEM Analysis:
    l_ref: [512, 4096] bf16 = 4MB
    r_ref: [1, 4096, 1024] bf16 = 8MB
    o_ref: [512, 1024] f32 = 2MB
    Input double-buffering: 2 * (4MB + 8MB) = 24MB
    Total: 24MB + 2MB = 26MB (Safely < 32MB)
    """
    return 512, 128, 1024

def gmm_tile(l_ref, r_ref, o_ref, *, bm, bk, bn, k):
    """
    Core GMM tile computation. 
    
    To improve performance over a single giant dot_general, we loop over 
    the contraction axis (k) in chunks of bk. This allows the Pallas 
    compiler to better pipeline VMEM loads and MXU computations, 
    reducing the likelihood of register spills and increasing overall 
    throughput.
    """
    # Initialize accumulator in float32 for precision and performance
    acc = jnp.zeros((bm, bn), jnp.float32)
    
    # Loop over the k-dimension in bk-sized chunks to allow pipelining
    for kk in range(0, k, bk):
        # Slicing MemoryRefs returns a value (tracer) for the specified region
        # l_ref is [bm, k], so we slice the contraction axis
        l_chunk = l_ref[:, kk : kk + bk]          # [bm, bk]
        
        # r_ref is [1, k, bn], slice the contraction axis (axis 1)
        r_chunk = r_ref[0, kk : kk + bk, :]       # [bk, bn]
        
        # Contract and accumulate in float32
        # dimension_numbers: (contracting axes, batch axes)
        # Contract axis 1 of l_chunk with axis 0 of r_chunk
        acc += jax.lax.dot_general(
            l_chunk, 
            r_chunk, 
            (((1,), (0,)), ((), ())), 
            preferred_element_type=jnp.float32
        )
    
    # Write the final float32 result back to the output Ref
    o_ref[...] = acc
```

import jax
import jax.numpy as jnp

def choose_tiles(m, k, n, num_groups):
    """
    Choosing bm=512 and bn=1024 to maximize MXU utilization while staying 
    under the ~32MB VMEM ceiling.
    
    VMEM Analysis:
    l_ref: [512, 4096] bf16 = 4MB
    r_ref: [1, 4096, 1024] bf16 = 8MB
    o_ref: [512, 1024] f32 = 2MB
    Input double-buffering: 2 * (4MB + 8MB) = 24MB
    Total: 24MB + 2MB = 26MB (Safely < 32MB)
    """
    return 512, 128, 1024

def gmm_tile(l_ref, r_ref, o_ref, *, bm, bk, bn, k):
    """
    Core GMM tile computation. 
    
    To improve performance over a single giant dot_general, we loop over 
    the contraction axis (k) in chunks of bk. This allows the Pallas 
    compiler to better pipeline VMEM loads and MXU computations, 
    reducing the likelihood of register spills and increasing overall 
    throughput.
    """
    # Initialize accumulator in float32 for precision and performance
    acc = jnp.zeros((bm, bn), jnp.float32)
    
    # Loop over the k-dimension in bk-sized chunks to allow pipelining
    for kk in range(0, k, bk):
        # Slicing MemoryRefs returns a value (tracer) for the specified region
        # l_ref is [bm, k], so we slice the contraction axis
        l_chunk = l_ref[:, kk : kk + bk]          # [bm, bk]
        
        # r_ref is [1, k, bn], slice the contraction axis (axis 1)
        r_chunk = r_ref[0, kk : kk + bk, :]       # [bk, bn]
        
        # Contract and accumulate in float32
        # dimension_numbers: (contracting axes, batch axes)
        # Contract axis 1 of l_chunk with axis 0 of r_chunk
        acc += jax.lax.dot_general(
            l_chunk, 
            r_chunk, 
            (((1,), (0,)), ((), ())), 
            preferred_element_type=jnp.float32
        )
    
    # Write the final float32 result back to the output Ref
    o_ref[...] = acc

# ===================== HARNESS SCAFFOLD -- appended verbatim =================
# Owns: the exclusive scan of group_sizes to row offsets, the group-ALIGNED row
# permutation (so no row tile ever straddles two experts -- the zero-size-group
# and boundary-tile correctness traps are the harness's, not yours), the
# pallas_call with scalar-prefetched per-tile expert ids, every BlockSpec, and
# the un-permute of the result.
import jax as _jax
import jax.numpy as _jnp
from jax.experimental import pallas as _pl
from jax.experimental.pallas import tpu as _pltpu


def _ru(x, m):
    return ((x + m - 1) // m) * m


def _tiles(m, k, n, g):
    bm, bk, bn = choose_tiles(int(m), int(k), int(n), int(g))
    bm = min(max(_ru(int(bm), 8), 8), 1024)
    bk = min(max(_ru(int(bk), 128), 128), int(_ru(k, 128)))
    bn = min(max(_ru(int(bn), 128), 128), 2048)
    return bm, bk, bn


def kernel(lhs, rhs, group_sizes):
    m, k = lhs.shape
    g, _k, n = rhs.shape
    bm, bk, bn = _tiles(m, k, n, g)
    np_ = _ru(n, bn)
    # static upper bound on the group-aligned row count: every expert wastes at
    # most bm-1 rows, and a zero-size expert wastes none.
    mp = _ru(m, bm) + g * bm
    n_row_tiles = mp // bm

    sizes = group_sizes.astype(_jnp.int32)
    starts = _jnp.concatenate([_jnp.zeros((1,), _jnp.int32), _jnp.cumsum(sizes)[:-1]])
    psizes = ((sizes + bm - 1) // bm) * bm                      # aligned sizes
    pstarts = _jnp.concatenate([_jnp.zeros((1,), _jnp.int32), _jnp.cumsum(psizes)[:-1]])

    # per PADDED row: which expert owns it, and which source row it is
    prow = _jnp.arange(mp, dtype=_jnp.int32)
    grp = _jnp.sum(prow[:, None] >= pstarts[None, :], axis=1) - 1   # [mp]
    grp = _jnp.clip(grp, 0, g - 1)
    off = prow - pstarts[grp]
    live = (off < sizes[grp]) & (prow < _jnp.sum(psizes))
    src = _jnp.where(live, _jnp.clip(starts[grp] + off, 0, m - 1), 0)

    lhs_p = _jnp.where(live[:, None], _jnp.take(lhs, src, axis=0), 0).astype(lhs.dtype)
    rhs_p = _jnp.pad(rhs, ((0, 0), (0, 0), (0, np_ - n))) if np_ != n else rhs
    # expert id of each ROW TILE: constant within a tile, by construction
    tile_grp = grp[::bm].astype(_jnp.int32)                     # [n_row_tiles]

    def _body(tg_ref, l_ref, r_ref, o_ref):
        gmm_tile(l_ref, r_ref, o_ref, bm=bm, bk=bk, bn=bn, k=k)

    out_p = _pl.pallas_call(
        _body,
        grid_spec=_pltpu.PrefetchScalarGridSpec(
            num_scalar_prefetch=1,
            grid=(n_row_tiles, np_ // bn),
            in_specs=[
                _pl.BlockSpec((bm, k), lambda i, j, tg: (i, 0)),          # lhs tile
                _pl.BlockSpec((1, k, bn), lambda i, j, tg: (tg[i], 0, j)),  # expert
            ],
            out_specs=_pl.BlockSpec((bm, bn), lambda i, j, tg: (i, j)),
        ),
        out_shape=_jax.ShapeDtypeStruct((mp, np_), _jnp.float32),
        compiler_params=_pltpu.CompilerParams(
            dimension_semantics=("arbitrary", "arbitrary"),
        ),
    )(tile_grp, lhs_p, rhs_p)

    # un-permute: original row r lives at padded row pstarts[grp_of_r] + (r - starts)
    row = _jnp.arange(m, dtype=_jnp.int32)
    rg = _jnp.sum(row[:, None] >= starts[None, :], axis=1) - 1
    rg = _jnp.clip(rg, 0, g - 1)
    dst = _jnp.clip(pstarts[rg] + (row - starts[rg]), 0, mp - 1)
    return _jnp.take(out_p[:, :n], dst, axis=0)

# Owns: the exclusive scan of group_sizes to row offsets, the group-ALIGNED row

# and boundary-tile correctness traps are the harness's, not yours), the

import jax as _jax
import jax.numpy as _jnp
from jax.experimental import pallas as _pl
from jax.experimental.pallas import tpu as _pltpu


def _ru(x, m):
    return ((x + m - 1) // m) * m


def _tiles(m, k, n, g):
    bm, bk, bn = choose_tiles(int(m), int(k), int(n), int(g))
    bm = min(max(_ru(int(bm), 8), 8), 1024)
    bk = min(max(_ru(int(bk), 128), 128), int(_ru(k, 128)))
    bn = min(max(_ru(int(bn), 128), 128), 2048)
    return bm, bk, bn


def kernel(lhs, rhs, group_sizes):
    m, k = lhs.shape
    g, _k, n = rhs.shape
    bm, bk, bn = _tiles(m, k, n, g)
    np_ = _ru(n, bn)

    # most bm-1 rows, and a zero-size expert wastes none.

    psizes = ((sizes + bm - 1) // bm) * bm                      # aligned sizes
    pstarts = _jnp.concatenate([_jnp.zeros((1,), _jnp.int32), _jnp.cumsum(psizes)[:-1]])

    grp = _jnp.clip(grp, 0, g - 1)

    lhs_p = _jnp.where(live[:, None], _jnp.take(lhs, src, axis=0), 0).astype(lhs.dtype)
    rhs_p = _jnp.pad(rhs, ((0, 0), (0, 0), (0, np_ - n))) if np_ != n else rhs

        gmm_tile(l_ref, r_ref, o_ref, bm=bm, bk=bk, bn=bn, k=k)

        _body,
        grid_spec=_pltpu.PrefetchScalarGridSpec(
            num_scalar_prefetch=1,
            grid=(n_row_tiles, np_ // bn),
            in_specs=[
                _pl.BlockSpec((bm, k), lambda i, j, tg: (i, 0)),          # lhs tile
                _pl.BlockSpec((1, k, bn), lambda i, j, tg: (tg[i], 0, j)),  # expert

        compiler_params=_pltpu.CompilerParams(
            dimension_semantics=("arbitrary", "arbitrary"),

    row = _jnp.arange(m, dtype=_jnp.int32)

## What you must define

```python
def choose_tiles(m, k, n, num_groups):
    """All PYTHON INTS. returns (bm, bk, bn):
       bm : rows of `lhs` per grid step   -> rounded up to a multiple of 8
       bk : YOUR contraction chunk        -> rounded to a multiple of 128
       bn : output columns per grid step  -> rounded to a multiple of 128
    bm and bn shape the grid and the VMEM footprint; bk is entirely internal to
    gmm_tile and does not touch the BlockSpecs."""

def gmm_tile(l_ref, r_ref, o_ref, *, bm, bk, bn, k):
    """ONE grid step: one row tile x one column tile, for ONE expert.
       l_ref [bm, k]     bfloat16  this tile's rows of lhs, the FULL k axis
       r_ref [1, k, bn]  bfloat16  this expert's weights, the FULL k axis,
                                   columns j*bn .. j*bn+bn-1
       o_ref [bm, bn]    float32   THE OUTPUT. You must write it.
       bm, bk, bn, k : Python ints.
    Every row in this tile belongs to the SAME expert -- the harness guarantees
    it -- and rows past the expert's true size are already zeroed, so you never
    check a boundary. `pl.program_id(0)` is the row tile, `pl.program_id(1)` the
    column tile."""
```

## Where the real decisions are

* **`bm` and `bn` are the VMEM budget.** `l_ref` is `[bm, 4096]` bf16 and
  `r_ref` is `[1, 4096, bn]` bf16, and Pallas double-buffers both: at
  bm=512, bn=1024 that is 8 MB + 16 MB before the accumulator, against a ~32 MB
  scoped ceiling. Too big is `CompileTimeScopedVmemOom` at compile time; too
  small wastes the MXU. This is the main thing you are choosing.
* **`bm` also sets the padding waste.** The harness aligns every expert to a
  multiple of `bm`, so a large `bm` with many small experts pads a lot of dead
  rows -- and those dead rows are multiplied anyway.
* **The k loop.** `k=4096` is fully resident. One `[bm, 4096] x [4096, bn]`
  `dot_general`, or a loop over `bk`-wide chunks accumulating into an f32
  register. The MXU wants big contractions; the register file does not.
* **Accumulation dtype and matmul precision.** `preferred_element_type=
  jnp.float32` over bf16 operands is the standard grouped-matmul choice.
* **Loop order.** You control only what is inside one tile, but within it the
  order of the k chunks and where you keep the accumulator are yours.

## Task semantics

`out[rows of group g] = lhs[rows of group g] @ rhs[g]`, at fp32 accumulation.
`group_sizes` sums to `m` and **zero-size groups are legal and do occur** (the
harness handles them; you never see one). The fp32 oracle is
`jax.lax.ragged_dot(lhs.astype(f32), rhs.astype(f32), group_sizes)`.

## Declared shapes

Expert widths are the real ones: k=4096, n=14336, num_groups=4.

    m=4096, num_groups=4, k=4096, n=14336, uniform group sizes  (scored)
    m=2048, num_groups=4, k=4096, n=14336, Zipf-skewed          (scored)
    m=3000, num_groups=4, k=4096, n=14336, Zipf-skewed          (HOLDOUT:
        logged, not scored, but it MUST still trace -- 3000 is deliberately not
        a multiple of any reasonable row tile)

Group sizes are freshly sampled from BOTH uniform and Zipf-skewed
distributions on every grading, so a kernel tuned for one balance cannot fake
a win on the other.

## Judge contract

- Exec'd, then `jax.export`ed once per declared shape, no data, no device.
- MUST contain a real `pallas_call` -- the scaffold's counts.
- Correctness on hidden seeds against the fp32 oracle (max and q99 tail, 1.5x
  the reference's own bf16 error). Non-finite fails automatically.
- Determinism: 5 runs, BITWISE identical. Compile budget 90 s for all shapes.
- Score: median wall-clock against the tuned Pallas megablox `gmm` that MaxText
  vendors. Note that the harness's group-aligned permutation costs a gather and
  a scatter that the baseline does not pay, so this denominator is harsh.
- Banned: `jax.experimental.pallas.ops.*` (it holds the megablox kernel),
  `tpu_inference`, `MaxText`, `maxtext`, `skyrl`, `recurrentgemma`, the arena's
  package.

## How much to write

Write **300-1200 tokens of code**. If you are past ~2000 you have started
rewriting the scaffold instead of choosing a strategy -- stop and delete it.

## Pallas dialect: the mistakes that actually killed candidates here

These are measured. Each line is the verbatim error and how many of the
previous attempts at these five kernels died on it.

1. **Names that DO NOT EXIST at this pin**, every one of them invented by a
   model on these exact tasks: `pl.load`, `pl.store`, `pl.swap`, `pl.Ref`,
   `pltpu.ANY`, `pltpu.TPUCompilerParams`, `jax.Shape`. A Ref is read and
   written by INDEXING, not by a load/store function. (The `pallas_call`
   keyword arguments that killed 4 more are the harness's problem here, not
   yours -- you do not write one.)

2. **Inside the kernel body you are handed Refs; a Ref is not an array.**
   Read `q_ref[...]`, `q_ref[0]`, `q_ref[:, :1]`; write `o_ref[...] = value`
   or `o_ref[0] = value`. A Ref has `.shape` and `.dtype` and NO `.astype` --
   read first, then cast.
3. **`pltpu.VMEM(shape, dtype)` is a SPECIFICATION, legal only inside
   `scratch_shapes=`.** Calling it in a kernel body returns a `MemoryRef`.
   Killed 9: `'MemoryRef' object does not support item assignment`. To
   accumulate inside a body use an ordinary value: `acc = jnp.zeros(...)`,
   `acc = acc + block`.
4. **A store into a Ref is not a broadcast** -- the right-hand side must
   already have the Ref's shape. Killed 6:
   `` Invalid shape for `swap`. Ref shape: (8, 4, 128) ... Value shape: () ``.
   Write `m_ref[...] = jnp.full(m_ref.shape, -1e30, m_ref.dtype)`, not
   `m_ref[...] = -1e30`.
5. **`dot_general`'s `dimension_numbers` is a PAIR OF PAIRS**, never flat.
   Killed 5 (`too many values to unpack (expected 2)`) plus several
   `dot_general requires ...`:
   `jax.lax.dot_general(x, y, (((1,), (1,)), ((), ())), preferred_element_type=jnp.float32)`
   -- that contracts axis 1 of x with axis 1 of y. WRONG: `((1,), (1,))`,
   `((1,), (1,), (0, 0))`.
6. **`@pl.when(cond)` is a DECORATOR that consumes the function and returns
   `None`.** Decorate and stop. Killed 2 (`'function' object is not iterable`,
   `'NoneType' object is not callable`). Never `with pl.when(...):`, never
   call the decorated name.
7. **Everything read out of a Ref is a TRACER.** `if`, `while`, `bool()`,
   `int()`, python `min`/`max` on one raise `TracerBoolConversionError`
   (killed 3). Use `jnp.where` for values and `@pl.when` for whole blocks.
   Python control flow on the STATIC ints (block sizes, page_size, head
   counts) is fine and unrolls at trace time.
8. **Mosaic tiling.** A block's last dim must be a multiple of 128 (or the
   whole dim) and its second-minor a multiple of 8 (or the whole dim). Killed
   1: `block shape are divisible by 8 and 128 respectively, or be equal to the
   respective dimensions of the overall array`. A `BlockSpec` block must also
   tile the operand: pad with `jnp.pad`, run the grid over the padded shape,
   slice the result back down. The HOLDOUT shape is in the declared set to
   force exactly this.
9. **`kernel` returns ONE array of the declared output shape and dtype**, not
   a tuple, not a Ref. Killed 2 (`function ... returned wrong type from jit`)
   and 2 more on layout (`cannot reshape array of shape (256, 4, 2560) into
   shape (4, 2560)`). If you scanned over time, `jnp.moveaxis` back before
   returning.
10. **You return VALUES, not Refs, and exactly the arity the scaffold
    unpacks.** Killed 5: `too many values to unpack (expected 2)`, and 3 more
    on `not enough values to unpack`. Read the scaffold above for what it does
    with your return.

One more, and unlike the rest it is not an API error -- it is the single
biggest cause of wrong ANSWERS here, so treat it as a rule.

**Keep every accumulator in float32. Pass `preferred_element_type=jnp.float32`
on every matmul.**

Your inputs are bfloat16 by contract. The MXU takes bfloat16 operands and sums
them in a wider float32 register -- but `jnp.dot`/`jnp.einsum` on two bfloat16
arrays RETURNS bfloat16, so that float32 accumulator is rounded away at the end
of every matmul unless you ask for it. bfloat16 carries about 3 decimal digits;
these kernels reduce over thousands of keys, pages, vocabulary entries or
timesteps, and the rounding compounds.

What a production TPU kernel does -- splash, vLLM-TPU, megablox, tokamax, all
of them -- is use bfloat16 ONLY as the matmul input format, because that is
what makes the MXU fast, and hold everything between matmuls at float32:
float32 logits, float32 softmax/LSE, float32 output accumulator. Narrow back to
bfloat16 only where a value is about to re-enter the MXU as an operand.

MEASURED against this judge's calibrated tolerance, across all five tasks: the
production path above lands at 0.4-0.7x of the budget, comfortably inside. The
same kernel written with the default dtypes -- bfloat16 logits, bfloat16
softmax, bfloat16 accumulator -- lands at 1.4x to 4.1x, i.e. it FAILS on
correctness while looking completely reasonable.

Do NOT reach for `precision=jax.lax.Precision.HIGHEST` instead. It is about 6x
the passes, no production kernel uses it for bfloat16 inputs, and buying
correctness with it just loses you the speed you are being scored on.
